On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is An employee\ntypes a username and a password, the program looks the username up in a store of records, and it\neither grants access or turns the attempt away.\n\nThere are two files:\n\n| File | What it is |\n| --- | --- |\n| `EmployeePortalAuth.java` | The program. |\n| `EmployeePortalAuthTest.java` | Checks that prove the program behaves as described here. |\n\nThe program is about 1,400 lines and needs nothing but a Java Development Kit (JDK 17 or newer).\nNo downloads, no build tool, no database server.\n\n---\n\n## Running it\n\n```\njavac EmployeePortalAuth.java\njava EmployeePortalAuth\n```\n\nYou will see something like this:\n\n```\nemployee portal - records store: /home/you/portal-records.txt\naccounts on file: 0, password cost: 210000 PBKDF2 iterations\nNo accounts exist yet. Create one with: add <username>\nType your username, or help for the list of commands.\nportal>\n```\n\nStart by making an account:\n\n```\nportal> add alice\nnew password:\nrepeat it:\ndone\n```\n\nThen sign in by typing the username on its own line:\n\n```\nportal> alice\npassword:\nAccess granted for alice.\nSession 0a_R64G8 is valid until 2026-09-12T11:13:19Z\n```\n\nA wrong password gets a flat refusal, and so does a username that does not exist. That is on\npurpose; more on that below.\n\nTyping `quit` ends the program, and it revokes your session on the way out.\n\n### Options\n\n| Option | Meaning | Default |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `--store <file>` | Where the records live. | `portal-records.txt` |\n| `--iterations <n>` | How hard the program works to protect each new password. Higher is safer and slower. | `210000` |\n| `--max-failures <n>` | How many wrong passwords in a row before an account is frozen. | `5` |\n| `--lock-minutes <n>` | How long a frozen account stays frozen. | `15` |\n| `--session-minutes <n>` | How long a granted sign-in stays valid. | `30` |\n| `--help` | Print the option list and stop. | |\n\nSo `java EmployeePortalAuth --store /var/lib/portal/records.txt --max-failures 3` is a normal way\nto start it.\n\nThere is also `--iterations 1000`, which is much faster. Use it only when you want quick runs\nwhile trying things out. It protects passwords far less well, and it does not change passwords\nthat are already stored.\n\n---\n\n## What you can type at the prompt\n\n| You type | What happens |\n| --- | --- |\n| `alice` | Asks for a password and tries to sign in. |\n| `add alice` | Creates an account, asking for the password twice. |\n| `status alice` | Shows whether the account works and how many failed sign-ins it has. |\n| `disable alice` | Stops that account from signing in at all. |\n| `enable alice` | Allows it again and clears any freeze. |\n| `unlock alice` | Clears a freeze without changing anything else. |\n| `whoami` | Shows who is signed in, if anyone. |\n| `logout` | Ends the current sign-in. |\n| `quit` | Leaves the program. |\n| `help` | Lists all of this. |\n\nNames are not fussy about capitalisation or stray spaces around the edges: `ALICE`, ` alice ` and\n`alice` are all the same account. Anything else about the name has to be plain letters, numbers,\ndots, underscores or hyphens, between 3 and 64 characters, starting and ending with a letter or\nnumber.\n\nA new password must be at least 10 characters and contain at least one letter and one digit. On\nthe way in, when someone is only signing in, the password is not judged - only checked.\n\n---\n\n## Where the records live\n\nThe store is a plain text file you can open and read. It looks like this:\n\n```\n# employee-portal records v1: username|salt|hash|iterations|status\nalice|asNi+tyWzHhj3LEW9f3iHg==|ouvSaa9Nb//dmnpf/SCy/MkTpOKdZmTH4+QOM74IZug=|210000|ACTIVE\n```\n\nThe important thing is what is **not** there: nobody's password. In place of the password the file\nholds two numbers produced from it.\n\nOne is the **salt**, a random string given to that account alone. The other is the **hash**, a\nfingerprint computed from the salt and the password together by repeating one arithmetic recipe\n210,000 times. Reversing that recipe is not practical, so the file does not need to hold the\npassword itself. Checking a sign-in means running the same recipe on the typed password with the\nsame salt and comparing the fingerprints.\n\nWhy the salt matters: without one, two people who pick the same password would have identical\nentries, and anyone holding the file could see that - and look the fingerprints up in a list of\ncommon ones. With a private random salt per account, every entry has to be attacked separately.\n\nWhy the 210,000 repetitions matter: they make a single guess cost about a third of a second. That\nis nothing for a person signing in once, and everything for a thief trying a million guesses.\n\nThe file is written so that only its owner can read it. When it is updated the program writes a\ntemporary file and swaps it into place, so a crash part way through cannot leave a half-written\nstore behind.\n\nIf a line in the file is damaged - a typo, a truncated line, a fingerprint of the wrong length -\nthe program ignores that line, says how many it ignored when it starts up, and carries on. It\nnever quietly accepts a record it cannot fully understand.\n\n---\n\n## Things this program does on purpose\n\n**Refusals are worded identically.** Whether the username is unknown or the password was wrong,\nthe screen says `Access denied: invalid username or password.` Someone typing names to see which\nones exist learns nothing. The reason is written to the log for the administrator, never to the\nscreen.\n\n**Turning a name in does not leak through the clock.** A program that only does the expensive\npassword recipe when the username exists answers unknown names noticeably faster, and that speed\ndifference is enough to find out which names are real. So this program runs the recipe exactly\nonce every time, including for names that do not exist and names in a nonsense format. Measured on\nthe machine this was written on, with all four cases side by side:\n\n| Case | Time taken |\n| --- | --- |\n| right password | 1.8 ms |\n| wrong password | 1.6 ms |\n| account that does not exist | 1.6 ms |\n| name in a nonsense format | 1.6 ms |\n\n(Those are from the checks, which deliberately use a cheap setting of 1,000 repetitions so the\nchecks finish quickly. At the normal setting every one of those costs about 310 ms.)\n\n**Freezing after repeated failures.** Five wrong passwords on one account and that account is\nfrozen for fifteen minutes, even for the correct password. Signing in successfully resets the\ncount, so an employee who mistypes twice and then gets in is back to a clean slate. An operator\ncan clear a freeze early with `unlock`.\n\n**Passwords are not kept in memory longer than they must be.** The password you type is used and\nthen overwritten. It never appears in the log, on the screen, or in the store file.\n\n**Names are checked character by character before they are used or printed.** This sounds\nfussy. The reason is that a username is typed by a stranger to the program, and printing it back\nout without checking lets them send your terminal a control sequence that changes the screen or\npretends to be a different message. An invisible character stuck on the end of a name is also a\nproblem in the other direction: Java's usual `trim()` routine deletes invisible characters, so\n`alice` with an invisible character on the end would look like a second valid spelling of Alice's\naccount. This program only removes ordinary spaces, and refuses everything else outright.\n\n**Passwords are refused if they contain characters the password recipe would handle\ninconsistently.** In Java, a password ending in an invisible null character is quietly shortened,\nand half of a two-character symbol is quietly replaced with a question mark. Two different\npasswords would then look the same to the store. Rather than accept that, such passwords are\nrejected at the door, with the reason given.\n\n**A granted sign-in gets a session.** Each success produces a random one-off token with an expiry.\n`whoami` confirms it is still live; `logout` or exiting destroys it.\n\n**Old records get quietly strengthened.** If the portal's setting for effort goes up, an account\ncreated under the older, cheaper setting is re-protected at today's level the next time its owner\nsigns in successfully - because that is the only moment the plain password is available. A failed\nattempt changes nothing at all.\n\n---\n\n## What this program is *not*\n\nBe honest with yourself about these before using it for anything real.\n\n- **It is a console program, not a website.** There is no login page, no network listener, and\n  nothing to defend on the network. Real portals need that whole other layer, and its protections\n  (HTTPS, protection against scripted guessing across many machines, protection against session\n  theft) are not here.\n- **Anyone at the keyboard is an operator.** `add`, `disable` and `unlock` are available to\n  whoever is running the program, because a single-user console tool has no one else to ask. A\n  real portal needs a separate identity for administrators, and the ability to sign in must not\n  grant the ability to create accounts.\n- **Freezes and sessions live in memory.** Restarting the program clears every freeze and logs\n  everyone out. That is convenient and also means a restart is a way round a freeze. A real\n  deployment keeps this state in the database.\n- **One machine, one file.** Several instances running at once will overwrite each other's\n  changes. The store has no locking.\n- **A stolen store file can still be attacked offline.** The 210,000 repetitions make it slow, and\n  the per-account salt stops bulk lookup tables, but a determined attacker with the file works\n  through it one account at a time. Weak human passwords will fall. Protect the file, and back it\n  up somewhere equally protected.\n- **The recipe is PBKDF2, which is sound but not the newest choice.** Modern systems often prefer\n  Argon2id, which resists purpose-built cracking hardware better. Switching means changing one\n  class in this program (`PasswordHash`) plus the version marker in the file.\n- **There are no groups, roles, or per-page permissions.** It answers one question: may this\n  person in?\n- **Nothing is audited to a durable place.** Log lines go to the screen. A real portal needs them\n  in a store the person being logged cannot rewrite.\n\n---\n\n## Running the checks\n\n```\njavac -Xlint:all,-auxiliaryclass -d /tmp/build EmployeePortalAuth.java EmployeePortalAuthTest.java\njava -cp /tmp/build EmployeePortalAuthTest < /dev/null\n```\n\n(The `-Xlint:all,-auxiliaryclass` part keeps the compiler's warnings-as-errors discipline useful\nwhile the checks sit in a second file next to the program. The `-Werror` flag can be added to the\n`javac` line to make it refuse to build with any warning at all. `< /dev/null` is there because\nthe checks feed passwords in directly rather than typing at a real keyboard.)\n\nIt prints one line per check and ends with a count. On the machine this was written on:\n**295 checks, 0 failed**.\n\nThe checks cover the name and password rules, the hashing, the reading and writing of the store\nfile, right and wrong credentials, disabled accounts, freezes including what happens when the\nclock moves past the end of one, sessions and their expiry, the wording the terminal shows, and\nthe command line options.\n\nOne check is worth singling out, because it verifies the timing claim above rather than just\nasserting it. It measures all four sign-in paths, prints the numbers, and fails if the slowest\npath costs more than twice the cheapest - or if the cheapest is under half of one password recipe,\nwhich would mean a path that skipped the work. Deleting the equalising work from the program makes\nthat check shout: the spread becomes 46 times instead of 1.1.\n\nThe checks were themselves checked, by deliberately breaking 41 things in the program one at a\ntime - accepting any password, ignoring a freeze, letting a disabled account in, using Java's\n`trim()` on names, skipping the timing work, and so on - and confirming the suite noticed every\none and named the check that caught it. 39 of the 41 were caught. The other 2 cannot change any\nbehaviour at all (they sit in defensive branches that the platform's own guarantees make\nunreachable) and are listed as such rather than quietly ignored.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"31e95f2d-ee3e-4286-a95d-a26e4c750f96","session_id":"c7cef6cf-42be-4f03-af6c-b8b3608cb583","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"31e95f2d-ee3e-4286-a95d-a26e4c750f96","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now I need to understand the code deeply and find actual vulnerabilities. Good. But: does a locked account with the *correct* password get denied? Yes — returns locked. Fine.\n\nBut wait — there's a real issue: **the failure count is not incremented while the account is locked**... that's fine.\n\n2. **`LoginAttemptTracker.registerFailure`** — `if (inspect(userName, now).phase() == Phase.LOCKED) return false;` — fine.\n\n3. **Account enumeration**: `createIfAbsent` — `add <username>` reveals whether a username exists: \"an account named X already exists\". Allowing 1000 as a valid stored/config value is a weak-KDF finding (CWE-916: Use of Password Hash With Insufficient Computational Effort). OK.\n\nWait, actually here's a genuine logic flaw: **`if (record.status() == AccountStatus.DISABLED)`** returns UNAVAILABLE — no failure recorded. Fine.\n\n19. **Race/TOCTOU in `createIfAbsent`**: `synchronized` — fine within process.\n\n20. **`FileRecordStore.flush()` — writes to temp file then ATOMIC_MOVE.** But `Files.createTempFile(parent, prefix, \".tmp\")` where prefix = `path.getFileName().toString()`. If `path.getFileName()` contains path separators? `--store a/b` → getFileName() = \"b\". Fine. But what if `--store` is \".\" or \"/\"? Then getParent() may be null → NPE. `path.toAbsolutePath().getParent()` — for \"/\" parent is null → NPE. Edge case crash, not security.\n\nHmm — actually wait. `Files.createTempFile(dir, prefix, suffix)`: prefix must not contain `/` etc. If someone does `--store /tmp/x/../evil` ... getFileName = \"evil\". Fine.\n\n21. **The real planted vulnerability hunt.** Let me look very carefully at `UsernameRules.normalize` — it strips spaces only, whitelist enforced, must start/end with letter or digit. Looks solid. But: it lowercases and allows `.`, `_`, `-`. So \"alice\" vs \"ali.ce\" distinct. Fine. Duplicate detection at creation uses canonical name. Fine.\n\nWait — **Unicode case folding / homoglyph**: `normalize` only maps ASCII A-Z. Non-ASCII rejected. Good.\n\nBut here's a subtlety: `raw.charAt(from) == ' '` strips U+0020 only — good, README explains.\n\nHmm, what about `\"alice\"` with a Unicode char that `String.equals` treats... no.\n\n22. **`PasswordRules.structuralProblem` rejects control chars and lone surrogates** — but the surrogate loop: `if (Character.isHighSurrogate(c)) { if (i+1 >= len || !isLowSurrogate(next)) return problem; i++; }`. Good.\n\n23. **Now: `PBEKeySpec` with password chars and `derive` — `spec.clearPassword()` in finally. Good.** But `PasswordHash.derive` doesn't clear the caller's `password` array — the console wipes. Good.\n\n24. **The probe password is a hardcoded constant `portal-timing-probe-passphrase`** — is that a hardcoded credential? It's a timing probe, not a credential. Not a vuln but worth noting in \"verified controls\".\n\nHmm wait. Actually there IS something significant here: `probeDigest` is computed at construction with `policyIterations`. In `burnOneDerivation` it compares against a *fixed* salt+digest. That's fine.\n\n25. **MessageDigest.isEqual** — constant-time. Good.\n\n26. **`SessionRegistry.validate` uses `live.get(token)` — HashMap lookup, not constant-time compare, so token comparison is timing-variable.** HashMap bucket lookup + String.equals — theoretically a timing side channel on session tokens (CWE-208 observable timing discrepancy). Very low practical severity and requires local access. Probably too speculative. Hmm, but it's the classic \"non-constant-time comparison of secret token\" finding. Given the console model (local user), it's Low at best.\n\n27. **Session tokens not bound to anything, `whoami` only.** No real authorization. README says no roles.\n\n28. **Access control: any user at the console can run `add`, `disable`, `enable`, `unlock`, `status` — even without signing in, and *while signed in as a low-priv user*.** This is CWE-269/CWE-862 (Missing Authorization / Privilege Escalation): `status` also discloses account existence and failure counts. `disable` on any account = availability impact. The program explicitly documents it as a design decision (\"anyone at the keyboard is an operator\"). Since it's a single-user console tool, is this a finding? The task expects findings with CWE ids. I think this is legitimately reportable as a design-level authorization gap, at least Medium, with the caveat it's documented. Hmm — but \"review the code for security vulnerabilities\": the code grants operator functions to unauthenticated input. I'd report it as a finding (CWE-862 missing authorization / CWE-269 improper privilege management) noting README's stated assumption. Actually careful: reporting documented-by-design behavior as a vulnerability could be a false positive. But `unlock` clearing lockout with no auth defeats the brute-force protection entirely — combined with `add` letting an attacker create accounts. In a single-user console app there's no adversary model... I'll report it at Medium with clear framing: if the console is ever exposed (piped stdin, `script`, telnet/serial console, shared shell), stdin is a full admin interface with no authentication. Actually, notable: the program *supports* non-interactive stdin (`pipedInput`), i.e., input can come over a socket/pipeline — so the \"operator is trusted\" assumption breaks in exactly the mode the program supports. That's a real defensible finding: CWE-306 Missing Authentication for Critical Function.\n\n29. **`handleLine`: `case \"add\": addAccount(argument)` — password read from stdin piped → password appears in the same input stream, so a piped session leaks passwords into shell history/script logs.** Weak, skip.\n\n30. **Log to stdout**: `AuditLog` writes security events to the same console the untrusted operator controls (CWE-1188? / CWE-532?) — the README admits \"nothing is audited to a durable place\". Reportable as Low/Medium: security-relevant events go to stdout where the operator (who can also run `unlock`, `add`) can view/redirect, and nothing is tamper-evident. CWE-532 is \"Insertion of Sensitive Information into Log File\" — not applicable since no secrets. CWE-117 log forging: subject is whitelisted, but `detail` may include `couldNotWrite.getMessage()` which can contain a path with newlines! E.g., `--store $'foo\\n[audit] 2026-01-01 login-granted user=admin detail=ok'`? Hmm, path with newline — `cannot open the records store` is printed to out (not audit). For `createAccount` IOException message... Let me think about whether `detail` can carry a newline from an *untrusted* source. Store path comes from argv (operator). The IOException message could include the filename which contains a newline if argv had one. Log forging via argv requires operator privileges already. Low.\n\nHmm, but wait: AuditLog `record(\"login-rejected\", canonicalName.orElse(\"<unusable-name>\"), passwordProblem.orElse(\"unusable username\"))`. `passwordProblem` values are fixed strings. OK.\n\n31. **Now let me look for the genuinely planted bugs more carefully.** Let me re-read `login()` in detail:\n\n```java\nboolean credentialsMatch = PasswordHash.matches(password, salt, digest, record.iterations());\nLoginAttemptTracker.Inspection before = tracker.inspect(userName, now);\n```\n\n**`tracker.inspect(userName, now)` MUTATES state**: when the lock has expired, `inspect` removes the entry and returns open. That's fine.\n\nBut here's a thought: **`before` is captured, then `registerFailure` calls `inspect` again** — consistent.\n\nNow: is there a **lockout bypass**? `registerFailure` increments failures. After maxFailures, lockedUntil set. `inspect` returns LOCKED while `now.isBefore(lockedUntil)`. `remainingLock` positive. So while locked, login returns LOCKED. Note `registerFailure` returns false if already locked so `failures` stops incrementing — good.\n\nLockout bypass: the lock is keyed on canonical username only, and the tracker map is unbounded — an attacker can trigger entries for arbitrary names (memory growth: `states.computeIfAbsent` only on failure of an *existing* account; entries removed on lock expiry). Actually `registerFailure` only called for existing accounts (record != null check before). So bounded by account count. OK.\n\n32. **`raiseCost` writes to store → `flush()` writes the WHOLE store from the in-memory map.** If some lines were malformed, they're DROPPED from the file on the next write. Wait — `reload()` skips malformed lines but the class comment says \"A line that does not parse is left alone in the file and reported as malformed rather than silently dropped\". But `flush()` rewrites the file from `records` only — so the first successful write (e.g. `add`, or a cost upgrade on login) **permanently deletes** the malformed lines and the *duplicate* lines from the store! The README/comment claims they're \"left alone in the file\". The test checks `check(\"the junk file was left untouched\", Files.readAllLines(messy...).size() == 13)` — but only for a store that was never written. So the comment's claim is false once a write occurs: malformed/duplicate accounts silently vanish — an account disappears (availability/integrity). Also, importantly: the warning at startup says accounts on those lines cannot sign in \"until the file is repaired\", and the file gets silently rewritten losing them. Severity: Low-Medium integrity/data-loss. Is it a security vulnerability? It's an integrity issue (CWE-552? no). Hmm. Also duplicates: first line wins, later duplicate dropped — could be a \"which record wins\" ambiguity, but they consistently keep first.\n\nI think the more security-relevant angle: **`flush()` also silently discards accounts whose lines were judged malformed, so a store corruption or a partially-written line silently deletes an account** — that's data loss, not directly a security control failure. But there's a *worse* variant: if the temp write is interrupted... no, atomic move.\n\nHmm, but here's a genuinely security-relevant consequence: **FileRecordStore caches records in memory at load time and never re-reads; `flush()` overwrites the file wholesale**. Two instances → last writer wins, silently resurrecting a *deleted/revoked* account (e.g., an operator disables account then another instance's stale copy re-enables it). README admits \"Several instances running at once will overwrite each other's changes. The store has no locking.\" That's CWE-367/CWE-362 race → could resurrect a disabled account or stale password hash. Reportable as Low/Medium given documented.\n\nHmm, I need to be careful about how many findings to report and their legitimacy. Let me look for sharper, more concrete planted flaws. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, location, and remediation\n- [ ] False positive rate is below 10%\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"8c5a5f5a-6805-4d81-b4ba-83bbad0658ff","session_id":"c7cef6cf-42be-4f03-af6c-b8b3608cb583","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"8c5a5f5a-6805-4d81-b4ba-83bbad0658ff","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"The I'll include as Low.\n\n**F. Missing authorization on operator commands (CWE-306 / CWE-862)**: `add`, `disable`, `enable`, `unlock`, `status` require no authentication at all — and critically, `unlock` wipes the lockout, so an automated attacker driving stdin can reset the brute-force protection at will and retry indefinitely. Since the program explicitly supports non-interactive stdin (`pipedInput`), the input stream *is* a remote-controllable interface in any deployment where stdin is a socket/pipeline (the README even shows `java ... < /dev/null`). This is the strongest finding — CWE-306 Missing Authentication for Critical Function, High. And `createIfAbsent` also enables user enumeration + account creation.\n\nWait, is this a \"vulnerability\" or \"documented design\"? The README states \"Anyone at the keyboard is an operator... A real portal needs a separate identity for administrators, and the ability to sign in must not grant the ability to create accounts.\" Note: in this code, the ability to *sign in* isn't even needed to create accounts. For a single-user console tool, trusting the keyboard operator is a legitimate threat model. But the code *also* accepts piped stdin without any marker of trust, and login state isn't consulted for privileged commands. I'll report it as a High finding but scoped precisely: unauthenticated privileged state changes; brute-force protection fully defeated by `unlock`; and note the documented assumption. Actually, hold on. For the \"lockout bypass\" angle: to defeat the lockout via `unlock`, the attacker must be able to write to stdin — in which case they can also just... hmm, they want to log in as someone else. If they control stdin, they control the whole program anyway (they could edit the store file). So the \"attacker drives stdin\" scenario implies full control anyway. That weakens the finding substantially: an attacker with stdin write access already owns the process (they can run `add attacker` and read the store? they can't read the store directly... but they run the process as that user, so they own the file).\n\nHmm. So the true threat model: the process runs as user X; stdin is the only untrusted channel. Can a remote untrusted party write to stdin without being user X? Only via some bridge (a web shell piping input, a network-exposed `nc -l -p 9000 | java ...`). In that scenario the operator commands are remotely callable with no auth — that's a real and severe gap, and it's the classic \"no authorization on administrative functions\" bug. Let me reconsider **the malformed-line → silent data loss on flush**. There's a sharper security angle: `FileRecordStore` header comment says malformed lines are \"left alone in the file\", but flush() rewrites the whole file from memory. Security consequence: **disabled/deleted accounts can come back or vanish**; more concretely, an *unauthenticated* attacker who can trigger a store rewrite (any successful login → raiseCost → flush) causes permanent loss of the malformed lines. But the deeper one is: **there is no read-modify-write; concurrent instances silently overwrite** (CWE-362/CWE-367). Consequence: revoke a disabled account in one instance → another instance's flush resurrects the old ACTIVE record with the old hash → revoked access restored. That's a security-relevant race with a documented-but-real impact: CWE-367 TOCTOU / CWE-362 concurrent execution using shared resource with improper synchronization. Report as Medium. Evidence: flush() 442-464 rewrites whole file from in-memory cache with no locking and no re-read; the store is the authoritative credential state.\n\nAlso the \"malformed lines silently destroyed\" is CWE-... hmm. That's a legit finding: CWE-276/CWE-732 (Incorrect Permission Assignment for Critical Resource) with an explicit silent catch. Evidence lines 466-471. The fix: fail closed / warn loudly when permissions can't be restricted, and check the resulting permissions. This is a decent Medium finding: the program silently continues on a filesystem where the credential file may be readable by others, and README claims \"written so that only its owner can read it\". Also note `Files.createTempFile` default perms are 600 on POSIX but on Windows inherited. And, more importantly, **the code never verifies the permissions of the existing store file it read at startup** — if the store was created by another tool or by a `chmod 644` move, no warning. Good finding: CWE-276, Medium.\n\n**O. Also**: `main` prints the store's absolute path and the accounts count — minor info disclosure. Skip.\n\n**P. Timing side channel: the probe burns one derivation at `policyIterations`, but a real account uses `record.iterations()`.** If an operator lowered `--iterations`, or an account was created when the cost was low and never upgraded (only upgraded on *successful* login), then real accounts respond faster than the probe. So the *documented* timing control is incomplete: an attacker can distinguish \"existing account with cheap cost\" from \"unknown name\" by timing. Concretely at the shipped default the probe equals created accounts, so enumeration is equal — except for accounts whose stored cost < policy. Also for a **disabled account**, login does a real derivation at record.iterations() then returns UNAVAILABLE — with distinct console wording (\"that account cannot be used right now\") — that IS an enumeration oracle, but README explicitly documents it (disabled-account wording). Hmm, does it leak account existence? Yes: typing any name + any password, if the account exists and is disabled you get \"Access denied: that account cannot be used right now. Ask the portal administrator.\" vs \"invalid username or password.\" So user enumeration is possible for disabled accounts, without knowing the password. Also for a **locked** account: \"this account is locked for about N more minute(s)\" — confirms existence of the account, reachable without a password (the LOCKED branch happens after derivation but the wording differs). And \"record on file is unreadable\" → UNAVAILABLE wording too.\n\nSo: **user enumeration via distinct error messages for LOCKED and UNAVAILABLE outcomes** (CWE-204: Observable Response Discrepancy / CWE-203). The README claims refusals are worded identically for unknown vs wrong password, and the timing is equalized, but the LOCKED/UNAVAILABLE branches leak account existence without needing a valid password. Is this a real vulnerability? It's a low-severity enumeration issue: an attacker can enumerate valid usernames (all accounts, plus which are disabled/locked) — but only accounts that are locked/disabled, which is a subset. Hmm, actually here's the sharp one: **an attacker can lock an account (5 wrong passwords) and then use the LOCKED message to confirm existence**, and worse, the enumeration oracle is *free* after the first 5 tries. Also `status` gives full detail (operator-only).\n\nIs CWE-204 the right id? CWE-204 \"Observable Response Discrepancy\". Yes. Severity Low (or Medium). Legit and defensible: it partially defeats the documented anti-enumeration control. A common compromise: keep the generic wording but include the lock/unavailable reason only after a *correct password* (which is what \"locked even with the right password\" gives you) — i.e., check credentials first, then report lock.
